二、主要稅目別變動 
（一）8 月實徵淨額與上年同月比較，以證券交易稅增加 262 億元、營業
稅、營利事業所得稅及綜合所得稅分別增加 68 億元、66 億元及 61
億元較多。 
（二）累計 1 至 8 月實徵淨額與上年同期比較，以證券交易稅增加 2,855
億元 、營利事業所得稅增加 1,599 億元及營業稅增加 906 億元較多。 
圖 2 主要稅目別變化 
(115 年 1-8 月) 
1.營利事業所得稅 
(1)8 月實徵淨額 132 億元，較上年同月增加 66 億元(+1.0 倍)，主因
大額退稅案件造成基期偏低所致。 
(2)累計 1 至 8 月實徵淨額 8,021 億元，較上年同期增加 1,599 億元
(+24.9%)，主因結算申報自繳稅額增加及延分期稅款陸續入帳。 
2.綜合所得稅 
8 月實徵淨額 890 億元，較上年同月增加 61 億元(+7.4%)，主因薪資
所得扣繳稅款增加；累計 1 至 8 月實徵淨額 7,790 億元，較上年同期
增加 757 億元(+10.8%)，主因結算申報自繳及盈餘分配 、 薪資所得等
扣繳稅款俱增所致。

(2)累計1至8月實徵淨額4,816億元 ， 較上年同期增加906億元(+23.2%)

(85%)及特銷稅(57%)等。

## Document 018: 2026-10-01_［みずほ経済EXPRESS］対日直接投資拡大で国内供給力の強化へ_f76b5ad3

Source PDF: all report pdf/2026-10-01_［みずほ経済EXPRESS］対日直接投資拡大で国内供給力の強化へ_f76b5ad3.pdf
Report source: Mizuho
Date: 2026-10-01
Pages: 5
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1

みずほ経済 EXPRESS

対日直接投資拡大で国内供給力の強化へ 
～戦略 17 分野でみた外資誘致の現状と課題～

調査部  エコノミスト 阿部大樹 
080-3709-6077 taiki.abe@mizuho-rt.co.jp 
■ 高まる国内投資の機運。 国内供給力強化に向けては対内直接投資の取り込みも重要に 
政府は2026年7月、 日本成長戦略会議において、 国内供給力の強化に向けた官民投資ロードマップを
決定した。とりわけ経済安全保障上の重要性や成長期待が高い17の戦略分野においては、2040年度ま
でに累計370兆円超の官民投資が想定されている（図表1）。日本政策投資銀行「2026年度設備投資計
画調査」では、大企業の約58％、中堅企業の約43％が戦略分野における国内投資を強化すると答えて
おり、多くの企業が政府の方針を前向きに受け止めていることがうかがえる。 
国内投資促進の主軸を担うのは政府や国内民間企業だが、国内供給力を強化していくうえでは、外
国資本の取り込み、すなわち対内直接投資の拡大を促すことも重要だ。対内直接投資とは、外国企業
が日本企業への出資や融資を通じて、日本国内の企業経営・事業に継続的に関わることを目的とした
投資のことをいう。政府も対日直接投資推進会議で、戦略分野ごとに有望な外国企業を発掘し誘致す
る方針を掲げている。 
対内直接投資は、 投資形態の違いから四つに分類できる （図表2） 。 このうち、 「合併・買収」 や 「企
図表1 戦略17分野 図表2 投資形態別分類

えてさらに概ね20兆円強の内数が投資額として想定されている
が、具体的な想定がわからない分については除外

### Page 2

2

業・財務再編」は国内供給力の強化につながらない可能性もあるが、「グリーンフィールド投資」と
「能力増強投資」は工場・生産ラインなど生産資本の増加をともない、供給網のレジリエンス向上や
国内の供給力強化につながりやすい形態だ。本稿では、これら二つの形態を合わせて「国内供給力強
化投資」と呼ぶこととする。以下では「国内供給力強化投資」に焦点を絞り、戦略分野ごとに直近5年
間の投資流入状況を確認したうえで、対内直接投資の今後の増加余地と誘致のあり方を検討する。 
■ AI・データセンター関連投資等のグローバルな機運が国内供給力強化投資をけん引  
日本の対内直接投資は、2010年代半ば以降、拡大傾向にある。国際収支統計で集計される対内直接
投資の動向を示した図表3をみると、 その力強い伸びが顕著に見て取れる。GDP比でも1.0％ 程 度（2025
年）まで増加しており、外国企業の国内投資志向が高まりつつある証左と言えよう。 
先行きは、さらなる対内直接投資の拡大が期待できそうだ。予定段階の個別投資案件を集計してい
るfDi Marketsのデータで国内供給力強化投資の動向を確認すると、2020年代に入って投資金額が大幅
に拡大していることがわかる （図表4）。同データでみられる投資拡大の動きは、クロスボーダー金融
取引の実績を計上している国際収支統計でも今後顕在化してくるとみられる。 
さらに、個別投資案件のデータを確認することで、国際収支統計では把握できなかった対内直接投
資の性質・内容を詳細に分析することが可能である。戦略分野別に国内供給力強化投資をみると、AI・
半導体分野や、データセンター関連が含まれるデジタル・サイバーセキュリティ分野で投資金額が大
きく、 かつ大幅な伸びが確認できる （図表5） 。 世界的なAIブームを受けたデータセンター関連投資の
活発化が背景とみられる。半導体製造設備やデータセンター関連の投資には巨額の資本が必要になる
ため、投資金額を件数で割った一件あたりの投資額が大規模化しやすく、大型投資案件が投資額全体
を押し上げていることがうかがえる。 
次に投資金額が大きかったのは資源・エネルギー安全保障・GX分野だ。具体的には、発電所などの
電力供給設備への投資案件が押し上げている。太陽光発電・洋上風力発電などの再生可能エネルギー
市場で海外企業の参入が増えた結果とみられ、脱炭素化を志向するグローバルな機運が追い風になっ
図表3 対内直接投資 図表4 国内供給力強化投資

ていると言える。加えて、電力需要の大きいデータセンターの立地を最適化するため、電力源と通信
インフラを一体的に整備する「ワットビット連携」が推進される中で、電力供給設備への投資需要が
高まっていることも当該分野の投資押上げに寄与しているとみられる。 
これらの分野では引き続きグローバルトレンドに沿った投資流入増が見込めそうである一方、造船
分野や防衛産業分野、量子分野、海洋分野の投資額は、増加しているものの依然として小規模だ。例
えば、量子分野は研究開発～実証段階の技術が中心の新領域であり、分野の将来的な成長期待は大き
い。現時点では収益化まで距離があることから、投資額の小ささ自体が問題とは言えないものの、米
国等と比べると日本の産業基盤は相対的に小さく、 スタートアップ企業の集積でも遅れを取っている。
さらに投資を呼び込むうえでは、こうした遅れを埋める手立てを講じることが必要不可欠であろう。 
また、造船分野では、2020年代に入ってから中国の存在感が一段と高まっている。日本の造船会社
による船舶の新規受注量は2000年代半ばから減少しており、世界の船舶建造量に占める日本のシェア
も低下傾向だ。背景には、中国と比べた事業所規模の小ささが指摘されており、生産コストなどの面
で水をあけられている。 
■ 幅広い分野での外国資本取り込みに向けて供給面 ・ 制度面のボトルネック軽減が重要 
戦略分野全体でみれば投資流入の堅調さが確認できた一方で、17の戦略分野それぞれにおいて投資
流入増を実現していく1ためには、 投資の門戸を開くだけでは不十分だ。 造船分野や量子分野に関して
指摘したような課題の解決に向けては、ボトルネックを軽減する地道な取り組みが必要であろう。ま
た、足元で急速に拡大しているデータセンター投資や再エネ関連投資についても、今後は成長の維持
が課題となる。 
外国資本取り込みにおける具体的なボトルネックとして、人材不足等の供給制約や複雑な制度、言
語障壁が挙げられる。日本国内の外資系企業を対象にした、日本貿易振興機構による「2025年度外資
系企業ビジネス実態調査」 では、 直近1～2年の日本のビジネス環境の課題について、 多くの企業が 「高
度人材等の確保」 と 「一般人材の確保」 を回答しているほか、 直近では 「オフィス・事業用地の確保」
図表5 戦略17分野別にみた国内供給力強化投資額の推移

（注） 各社資料で確認できなかった具体的な投資額はfDi Marketsによる推計値。 期間内に国内供給力強化投資が確認できなかった分野につい
ては除外。各社の個別投資プロジェクトの情報及び、日本成長戦略会議資料を基に、みずほ総合研究所調査部が分類 
（出所）fDi Marketsより、みずほ総合研究所調査部作成

### Page 4

4

を回答する企業の割合も高まっている。こうした人材・立地面等の供給制約は、外資の取り込みを進
めるうえで課題となる。 
また、制度面について、日本貿易振興機構による「ジェトロ対日投資報告2018」では、外国企業が
日本でビジネス展開するうえでの阻害要因として「行政手続きの複雑さ」や「許認可制度の厳しさ」
等が挙げられ、外国企業にとって日本のビジネス環境への適応が容易ではないことがうかがえる。な
お、 同調査では 「外国語によるコミュニケーションの難しさ」 が2位にランクするなど、 言語障壁も外
国企業の参入を阻む一因だ。 
供給面・制度面のボトルネック解消に向けた対応として、人材不足については、大学等の研究・教
育機関との連携強化を通じた国内人材の育成・確保や、高度外国人材の受入強化による対応が考えら
れる。また、用地不足に対しては、産業用地の確保や空き家・空き地の有効活用を通じて、外国企業
が拠点を設立しやすい環境を整備することが重要だろう。 
制度面の対応としては、まず、手続きの一本化が挙げられる。現状、外国企業等の投資申請に際し
ては担当省庁・公的機関と個別にやり取りするケースがあり、外国企業の手続き負担は小さいとは言
えない。 この点、OECD（2026） は外国企業 ・ 投資家と関係省庁等との調整を担う 「ワンストップ窓口」
の機能強化を提言している。 言語障壁については近年通訳や英訳による手続き支援が進みつつあるが、
主要な政策等については英語アナウンスを適時提供するなど、言語障壁軽減に向けたさらなる取り組
みが求められる。

［参考文献］ 
OECD (2022), FDI Qualities Policy Toolkit, OECD Publishing 
OECD (2025), OECD Benchmark Definition of Foreign Direct Investment,  5th edition, OECD 
Publishing 
OECD (2026), OECD Economic Surveys: Japan 2026, OECD Publishing

### Page 5

5

1 コンテンツ産業のような日本企業に競争力がある分野では、外国資本の取り込みによる事業拡大も期待される一方、独自技術
や知的財産の流出リスクには留意し、慎重に議論を進める必要がある。

市場焦點
美國
• 9月CPI年增3.7%、PPI年增6.3%，雙雙高於前期 ，能源價格的急升是通膨回升的主要推力 。
雖然核心CPI年增率僅升至2.6%，但住房與服務價格仍維持黏著，使通膨降溫過程出現反覆；
同時PPI增幅明顯高於 CPI，反映企業成本壓力偏高 ，未來不排除進一步向終端價格傳導 。整
體而言，CPI與PPI同步回升反映通膨降溫進程仍存在不確定性 ，進而令市場重估通膨回落速
度與對Fed貨幣政策路徑的審慎預期。
AI
• AI雲端業者 CoreWeave的5年期CDS利差一度突破 1,140個基點 ，旗下兩檔債券殖利率逼近
15%，相關賣壓並進一步蔓延至股票市場 。全球最大半導體設備商ASML表示，客戶已開始洽
談2029至2030年的訂單；晶圓代工龍頭台積電財報亦優於市場預期 。與此同時，台灣出口增

速創3月以來新高，韓國9月半導體出口年增逾260%，顯示半導體需求持續強勁，預期第三季
相關企業獲利仍有望維持強勁。
中國
• 9月通膨加速主要源於中東能源衝擊及 AI硬體需求，能源和科技領域之外 ，普遍性的通縮壓力
仍在，彭博表示以GDP平減指數衡量 ，經歷三年下滑後 ，整體經濟預計將在 2026年走出通縮 ，
投資上短期可聚焦，受惠AI資本支出與能源價格上行的半導體、伺服器及能源相關族群。

美股獲利引擎轉向半導體/核心AI與能源
• 雖然標普500指數在2026年第三季展現出獲利韌性 ，但隨著油價上漲侵蝕利潤率 、消費者受通膨壓抑 ，以及分析師下調未
來預測動能，市場未來的容錯空間正在縮小。
• AI與半導體仍為獲利領頭羊：科技與半導體持續為標普500指數獲利成長的核心動力，其中約20家企業組成的核心AI一籃子
股票(權重約34.6%)對指數獲利的貢獻度已超過科技七雄(Mag 7)，且此優勢預計將至少延續至2028年底。
• 油價推升能源產業，接棒成為強棒：受到近期國際油價上揚影響，能源產業獲利預測顯著上修，預計獲利成長勁道將延續至
2027年初。在接下來的三個季度中，能源板塊的收益成長表現預計將超越科技七雄。
• 消費與運輸業面臨成本壓頂：相較於科技與能源的強勁，必需消費品與交通運輸業正承受顯著壓力。例如，達美航空受高燃

料成本拖累，而Nike與百事可樂則反映出消費者因持續通膨而縮減支出的困境。
7月以來EPS和營收成長預期修正
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.10.16
剔除能源、科技七雄和AI一籃子股票後的指數收益成長

利潤率高檔盤整、關稅退款帶來短補，但動能已見放緩
• 利潤率高檔盤整，淨利潤率提升受限：標普500成分股第二季營運利潤率(18.7%)與淨利潤率(15.6%)均創下新高。雖然第三
季營運利潤率預估可進一步提升至19.4%，但淨利潤率預計微幅上升至15.8%，顯示整體企業淨利潤的擴張動能正漸趨平緩 。
• 700億美元關稅退款提供短線補藥： 受惠於美國政府依據國際緊急經濟權力法 (IEEPA)的追償退稅，估計仍有至少700億美元
的關稅款將退還給標普 500成分股(主要集中於工業、非必需消費及醫療保健等 )，這預計能為2026年標普500指數每股收益
(EPS)成長帶來約235個基點、利潤率提升約34個基點的短期提振。
• 上修動能開始轉弱：雖然整體財報指引動量仍維持正值，但過去六週內，分析師對未來12個月EPS與收入的修正動能出現頻

繁下修，這意味著市場先前的極度樂觀情緒正在消退，未來財報季度要優於預期的門檻正在提高。
• 小結與投資建議： 標普500指數短期內靠著半導體 /AI與能源雙引擎拉抬 ，加上關稅退款的一次性利多注入 ，下半年整體獲
利依然具備支撐。然而，高油價帶來的營運成本上升 ，以及終端消費力道的疲軟 ，正逐漸侵蝕非科技/非能源企業的利潤 ，
而大盤廣度不足(僅少數板塊支撐獲利)，代表未來投資風險正逐漸集中 ，但AI算力需求仍屬硬性需求，但波動加劇下，投資
建議以定期定額方式投資美股核心AI與半導體龍頭股票。

美國9月CPI、PPI 同步升溫，能源衝擊擴大通膨與利率風險
• 能源價格急升，消費通膨再升溫：美國9月CPI月增0.6%、
年增3.7%，高於前月 3.4%，其中汽油月增 9.1%、能源
價格月增 5.2%，汽油單項即貢獻整體 CPI當月漲幅逾半；
另一方面，核心CPI月增0.3%、年增2.6%、住房指數月
增0.4%，顯示能源雖是本次通膨回升主因 ，但住房及部
分服務價格仍具黏著性，令通膨降溫進程面臨反覆。
• 上游價格大幅攀升 ，成本傳導風險升高： 9月PPI最終需
求月增1.3%、年增6.3%，創2022年3月以來最大增幅，
其中最終需求商品月增 2.6%，能源價格大漲 10.8%，汽
油價格更跳升 20.3%；核心PPI月增0.5%、年增5.0%，
顯示價格壓力不僅來自能源 ，也涉及部分商品與服務環

節；後續是否進一步推升至 CPI，仍須觀察企業成本轉
嫁程度及需求變化。
• 市場看法： 通膨回升恐提高 Fed再度升息機率 ，並延長
高利率維持時間 ，甚至不排除進一步緊縮的可能 ，短期
有利支撐美債殖利率及美元表現 ，不過股、債市後續走
勢仍將受到貨幣政策預期與風險情緒變化影響 。資產配
置建議可關注具成本轉嫁能力 、現金流穩健的能源類股；
債券則宜以短中天期 、高信用評等標的為主 ，以降低利
率敏感度。黃金可作為分散地緣政治及通膨風險的輔助
配置，但不宜將任何單一資產視為必然受惠標的。
CPI 與核心 CPI 走勢變動

回
顧
供給面推動中國9月CPI升0.9%
• 供給面推動中國9月通膨復甦：中國9月通膨數據顯示，CPI年增率由8月的0.8%升至0.9%，連續兩個月擴大，但低於市場預期
的1.0%，物價上漲主要受到能源及旅遊價格帶動，而食品價格跌幅進一步擴大，使整體通膨壓力仍相對溫和，扣除食品與能源
後，核心CPI維持在1.0%，反映內需需求尚未明顯升溫 。另一方面，PPI年增率由3.8%大幅升至5.0%，優於市場預期，且月增
率達1.2%，創4月以來最大漲幅，顯示上游原物料及工業品價格回升，須關注未來是否逐步傳導至消費端價格。
• 展望後市： 9月通膨加速主要源於中東能源衝擊及 AI硬體需求 ，能源和科技領域之外 ，普遍性的通縮壓力仍 在，彭博表示 以
GDP平減指數衡量，經歷三年下滑後，整體經濟預計將在2026年走出通縮，值得注意的是，這種供給面驅動的通膨復甦，將降

低官方推出需求刺激措施的緊迫感 ，而要反轉通縮循環正需要官方舉措，投資上短期可聚焦受惠AI資本支出與能源價格上行的
半導體、伺服器及能源相關族群。
資料來源：彭博，2026.10.14
通膨集中於能源和AI相關行業

過去18個月來，新興市場股票表現亮眼，累積報酬率超過標普500指數
的兩倍
• 過去18個月來，新興市場股票表現亮眼，累積報酬率超過標普500指數的兩倍：主因為獲利帶動成長，整體而言，新興市場企
業獲利表現強勁 ，而且仍持續加速成長 ，高盛預估2026年新興市場企業盈餘成長率將達 55%，超過標普500企業盈餘成長速
度的兩倍，且新興市場股票估值較美國股市存在超過40%的折價空間，評價相對具吸引力，今年以來流入新興市場股市的資金
已接近500億美元，創下過去20年來最強勁紀錄之一。更重要的是新興市場國家目前貢獻了全球近 80%的AI硬體出口。隨著AI
產業持續發展，受惠範圍有望從少數龍頭企業擴大至更多國家及公司，特別是那些能夠協助解決供應鏈瓶頸的企業。
• 結論：後市來看在AI產業趨勢、相對低估的評價水準，以及強勁企業獲利成長三大因素支撐下，新興市場股票未來一年仍具備

進一步上漲的潛力，有機會延續目前的強勢表現。

AI投資支撐美國成長，融資需求升高使長端利率與信用風險並存
• AI基礎建設持續擴張 ，成為企業投資重要動能 ：大型雲端業者
持續投入資料中心 、半導體、網路設備與電力基礎設施 ，帶動
相關產業資本支出與供應鏈需求 。摩根大通資產管理指出 ，納
入資料中心開發商及半導體投資後，AI 基礎建設至 2030 年的
累計投資需求可能達約 5兆美元 ，顯示AI投資循環仍具規模 ，
但實際經濟貢獻與企業獲利 ，仍取決於投資效率及商業化成果 。
• 外部融資需求增加 ，債券供給與投資回報成關鍵： 隨AI投資規
模擴大，大型科技企業逐步增加公司債與外部融資 ，在企業債
與公債供給同步增加下 ，長天期利率面臨上行壓力 。若未來AI
獲利成長未能跟上資本支出擴張速度 ，可能壓縮自由現金流與

資本報酬率，進一步擴大信用利差 ，並提高高估值科技股的評
價修正風險
• 市場觀察：AI基礎建設仍是未來數年推動全球科技投資與企業
數位轉型的成長主軸，相關需求具備中長期發展空間，而2026
年市場已逐漸轉向檢視投資回報率 、現金流創造能力及融資結
構是否具備可持續性 ，因此單純依賴資本支出擴張已不足以支
撐所有AI概念股評價 。建議配置可兼顧具獲利與現金流支撐的
AI供應鏈，以及高信用評等 、中短天期債券；對長天期債券則
須評估供給與利率風險 ，並審慎檢視高負債 AI企業的償債能力
及投資回報。
資料來源：Goldman Sachs，永豐金證券， 2026.10.12

AI熱潮、高債務與網路威脅，IMF示警三大金融穩定風險升高
資料來源：Bloomberg Finance L.P， Dealogic，Zero day Clock and IMF staff calculations.， 2026.10.13
AI相關風險持續升溫 主權債券殖利率攀升 網路資安威脅升高
大型雲端科技企業債券發行規模
（占投資等級公司債市場比重) 10年期公債殖利率平均變動幅度 零時差漏洞占比（%）
• AI估值高度集中，市場重定價風險升溫：IMF於10月《全球金融穩定報告》指出，AI相關投資已成為全球成長的重要支撐，
但資金與漲勢高度集中於少數企業及市場，一旦獲利成長不如預期或估值遭重新檢視，可能引發科技股修正並透過風險偏好
下降擴散至整體金融市場。
• 通膨與財政疑慮恐推升債券市場波動： IMF認為，若能源價格推升通膨壓力或市場對財政永續性產生疑慮 ，主權債殖利率可

能進一步上升，進而提高全球融資成本與資產重估風險；在高利率與高債務環境下，金融條件快速緊縮的情況將明顯提高。
• 網路安全風險成為潛在威脅： 隨金融體系數位化程度提高 ，IMF將大型網路攻擊列為重要風險來源之一 。若攻擊事件波及支
付系統、金融基礎設施或雲端服務平台，不僅可能影響市場運作效率，也可能透過信心衝擊與流動性壓力擴大金融市場波動。
• 投資建議：市場成長動能仍受AI支撐，但在金融穩定風險上升情境下，建議採取「AI受惠股＋高品質債券＋防禦型資產」的
槓鈴策略；核心配置可聚焦全球AI基礎建設供應鏈及投資等級債，藉由企業長期獲利成長與債券避險功能，同時因應估值修
正及金融條件收緊風險。

• CoreWeave信用風險亮紅燈：市場憂心雲端業者在算力租賃業務產生足夠回報前，恐難以負擔龐大債務，導致信用風險升
溫。上週，AI雲端業者CoreWeave的5年期信用違約交換（CDS）利差一度突破1,140個基點，升至通常反映嚴重信用壓力
的水準；旗下兩檔債券殖利率逼近15%，其中2032年到期的無擔保債券於上週三跌破面值的80%，相關賣壓並進一步蔓延
至股票市場。
• AI半導體產業的基本面仍具韌性：全球最大半導體設備商艾司摩爾（ASML）表示，客戶已開始洽談2029至2030年的訂單；
晶圓代工龍頭台積電財報亦優於市場預期。與此同時，台灣出口增速創3月以來新高，韓國9月半導體出口年增逾260%，顯
示半導體需求持續強勁，預期第三季相關企業獲利仍有望維持強勁。

美銀美林調查指出，全球經理人對景氣與股市仍維持偏多立場
資料來源：BofA Global Fund Manager Survey，2026.10
• 經理人樂觀情緒維持高檔： 美銀美林10月全球基金經理人調查顯示 ，市場風險偏好仍高 ，平均現金水位維持在 3.9%的低檔，
科技股配置則升至2020年8月以來最高，反映經理人對AI產業及企業獲利前景仍具信心 。然而，整體經濟成長預期已轉趨保
守，淨4%的受訪者預期未來 12個月經濟成長放緩 。極端風險方面，債券殖利率無序攀升仍居首位 ，占比31%；AI泡沫及通
膨再起分別為23%及20%。此外，39%的經理人認為，大型雲端業者資本支出是系統性信用事件的主要潛在來源 ，顯示市場
焦點已從AI需求成長延伸至龐大資本支出的獲利回收能力。

• 美國公債殖利率走高 ，債券的收益吸引力逐漸回升： 調查顯示，37%的受訪者表示，當美國10年期公債殖利率升至 6%時，
才會考慮由股票轉向債券；另有19%認為目前殖利率已具吸引力，顯示部分經理人已開始重新評估固定收益資產的配置價值 。
不過，債券整體仍處淨減碼，且經理人對科技股的配置持續升高 ，反映市場尚未全面轉向防禦。後續仍須關注美債殖利率變
化、AI資本支出的投資回報，以及企業獲利能否支撐偏高的市場評價。
資料來源：BofA Global Fund Manager Survey，2026.10

台積電財報佳卻未激勵股價，短線面臨總經干擾
• 台積電Q3營收、獲利及EPS同步改寫單季新高：營收年增50.9%、季增17.6%，稅後純益7,630.6億元，年增68.7％、季增8
％；每股稅後純益(EPS)29.43元，整體優於市場預期，但毛利率66.4％較第二季的67.7％下降。
• 財測展望：第四季財測營收預估 523~543億美元，季增上看15％、年增65％，毛利率預估65%~67%，反映高階晶片需求
依然強勁。全年展望方面，預期2026年美元營收年增約44%，較上季預估的「略高於40%」說法更加明確。此外，重申全
年資本支出600~640億美元不變。
• 市場反應：台積電法說會隔日股價小幅下跌，法說會未出現激勵行情，主要原因包含美國通膨升溫壓力、外資連續調節部位，

以及毛利率略為下降。短線籌碼面可能仍會調整，但台積電董事長魏哲家在法說會中強調，第二波AI需求正強勁興起，台積
電正透過多種途徑全力擴充產能，顯示高階晶片前景明確，中長期具投資價值，多家投資機構也紛紛上調目標價。
資料來源：台積電法說會，兆豐銀行整理，2026.10.15。
法說會重點整理 說明
AI需求持續升溫
魏哲家坦言，目前仍難以判斷晶圓供需缺口將擴大或縮小，因為
客戶需求持續增加，台積電也正積極提高產能。儘管消費性電子
市場仍受到零組件漲價及總體經濟不確定性影響，但先進製程需
求依然強勁，公司預估2026年全年美元營收將年增約44％。
擴廠進度
(1)台南科學園區新建3奈米廠預計2027年下半年量產，並決定在
台南再增建一座3奈米廠，規畫2028年投入量產。
(2)美國亞利桑那州第二座晶圓廠已完成廠房興建，預計2027年
第三季量產，時程較原先規畫略為提前；日本第二座晶圓廠則將
導入3奈米製程，預計2028年量產。
台積電證實洽談Terafab
合作
魏哲家強調，目前相關合作仍在討論階段，尚未有結論。不過，

無論未來採取何種合作模式，晶圓廠都必須由台積電負責營運。
電力供應疑慮
美國電力供應情況是公司評估AI長期需求的重要因素之一，因此
台積電進一步確認當地電力供應狀況，目前相關研究及評估結果
均良好，尚未發現明顯問題。

• 全球經濟正處於「韌性仍在、風險升高」的階段：國際貨幣基金組織(IMF)指出，AI投資熱潮持續推動美國、台灣等國家成
長，但中東地緣政治衝突、高通膨風險、政府債務問題與AI泡沫化疑慮，也同時為市場埋下不確定因素。
• 市場永遠充滿雜音，定期定額紀律投資更具優勢：本許多人認為定期定額的核心在於「平均成本」，但更重要的是幫助投資
人克服人性。市場上漲時持續投入，可以參與企業獲利成長帶來的成果，市場下跌時持續扣款，則能以較低價格累積更多單
位數。換句話說，定期定額讓投資人不需要每天判斷：明天會不會跌？現在是不是太貴？要不要先停扣觀望？長期而言，紀

律投資往往比短期擇時的成效更佳。
• AI時代更適合定期定額：儘管IMF認為人工智慧發展為全球經濟成長帶來貢獻，但同時也警告，若市場對AI未來獲利能力過
度樂觀，不排除出現股市估值修正。根據歷史經驗，每一次市場重大修正往往也孕育下一輪成長契機，不論AI發展是否出現
泡沫，善用定期定額策略可以在機會與風險之間達到平衡。
韌性仍在、風險升溫，紀律投資更穩健
任一月份定期定額進場投資試算
註：以MSCI全球指數2010/1/31~2025/12/31試算。假設定期定額每月月底投入、
原幣計算。指數資料試算僅供參考，非投資商品之實際績效。資料來源Bloomberg。
短進短出錯失行情的代價大

1. 本資料內容僅供內部同仁及客戶參考，非作為任何金融商品或服務之購買或銷售之推介，不得視為買賣有價證券或其他金融商品之要約或要約之引誘。
2. 本資料提及之經濟走勢預測不必然代表基金之績效，基金投資風險請詳閱基金公開說明書。
3. 基金經理公司以往之經理績效不保證基金之最低投資收益；基金經理公司除盡善良管理人之注意義務外，不負責本基金之盈虧，亦不保證最低之收益 ，投資人申購
前應詳閱基金公開說明書。
4. 本資料所載內容如涉及商品資訊 ，商品相關資訊仍以經核可之條款及文宣 (DM)為主，本行網站已備有公開說明書可供查閱基金應負擔之費用及相關投資風險 。投
資人申購基金係持有基金受益憑證，而非本資料提及之投資資產或標的。
5. 本資料所載內容取自本公司相信可靠之資料來源，且為特定日期之判斷，本公司不保證其即時性、精確性及完整性，亦不提供或嘗試遊說客戶做為投資之判斷依據。

資料內容邇後若有變更，本公司將不做預告或主動更新。投資人於決策時應審慎衡量本身風險，如因信賴本資料內容而做出或改變投資決策，須自行承擔結果，本
公司及本公司所屬員工，均不負任何損害賠償及其他法律上之責任。
6. 本資料內容之著作權屬兆豐銀行所有，禁止就所載資料任何形式之抄襲、引用或轉載。
7. 本資料所載部分基金涉及非投資等級債，投資人投資以非投資等級債券為訴求之基金不宜占其投資組合過高之比重 。基金經金管會核准，惟不表示絕無風險。由於
非投資等級債券之信用評等未達投資等級或未經信用評等，且對利率變動的敏感度甚高，故本基金可能會因利率上升、市場流動性下降，或債券發行機構違約不支
付本金、利息或破產而蒙受虧損。部分基金不適合無法承擔相關風險之投資人。基金經理公司以往之經理績效不保證基金之最低投資收益；基金經理公司除盡善良
管理人之注意義務外不負責本基金之盈虧，亦不保證最低之收益，投資人申購前應詳閱基金公開說明書。
8. 基金年化配息率計算公式：每單位配息金額 ÷除息日前一日之淨值×一年配息次數×100%。「年化配息率」為估算值。當月配息率計算公式：每單位配息金額 ÷除

息日前一日之淨值×100%。
9. 基金配息率不代表基金報酬率，且過去配息率不代表未來配息率；基金淨值可能因市場因素而上下波動。投資人因不同時間進場，將有不同之投資績效，過去之績
效亦不代表未來績效之保證。本資料如以基金過去績效進行模擬投資組合之報酬率時，僅為歷史資料模擬投資組合之結果，不代表本投資組合之實際報酬率及未來
績效保證，不同時間進行模擬操作，其結果亦可能不同。
10. 部分基金配息前未先扣除應負擔之相關費用。基金的配息可能由基金的收益或本金中支付。任何涉及由本金支出的部份，可能導致原始投資金額減損。各基金由本
金支付配息之相關資料，投資人可至各投信基金公司及總代理人網站查詢。
11. 屬基金資訊觀測站ESG基金專區所列之基金，有關基金之ESG資訊，投資人應於申購前詳閱基金公開說明書或投資人須知所載之基金所有特色或目標等資訊。

For the month of October 2026, we estimate AirTAC’s monthly revenue will decrease 
to Rmb729mn (-10% MoM, +16% YoY)/NT$3,447mn (-10% MoM, +28% YoY) 
We expect October revenue will go down by 10% MoM to Rmb729mn/NT$3,447mn, 
mainly due to 19% fewer working days in Mainland China. However, we expect to see 
improvement in daily pneumatic revenue (Rmb32.3mn in Oct vs. Rmb 29.4mn in Sept) as 
the improvement in daily pneumatic revenue is driven by the strong growth from key 
customers, including electronics, followed by machine tools and battery, and we further 
expect demand to improve following the end of low season.

Earnings and TP revisions 
We revise down our 2026E earnings estimates by 1% to factor in actual 3Q26 revenue 
(revise down 2026 revenue by 1%). We revise down our 2027E/2028E earnings estimate 
by 1%/1% to factor in a more conservative assumption on battery and linear guide 
business (revise down AirTAC revenue by 1%/1% in 2027E/2028E). We continue to see 
the company focusing on cost control as well as experiencing a better product mix 
outlook, but we expect some offset by rising material cost (revise down 2026E/2028E 
OPM by 0.1/0.1ppt).

Maintain our Buy rating on AirTAC, with a new 12m TP of NT$1,895 (from NT$1,925), 
based on an unchanged target P/E of 26x on 2027 EPS.

Investment view: Accelerating monthly YoY from smaller segments could be a surprise. 
Maintain positive view on AirTAC 
We reiterate our Buy rating on AirTAC as we continue to believe the company is a key 
beneficiary of the factory automation/industrial automation localization trend in 
mainland China, and we expect the company will also benefit from the prolonged 
upcycle supported by the ongoing government stimulus policies. We see multiple key 
end-segments’ performance tracking ahead of the company’s 2026 full year guidance of 
20%+ YoY growth (see here), with battery/textile/machine tools/electronics delivering 
55%/46%/41%/23% YTD YoY growth while total revenue in Rmb is tracking at 23% YTD 
YoY growth. 
We continue to see solid monthly YoY growth from smaller segments, such as energy 
lighting and textile. However, the battery segment, which is the key growth driver in 
2025 (with 89% YoY in 2025), is seeing its first negative monthly YoY growth since Oct 
2024, while other major contribution sectors, such as electronics and automotive are 
seeing decelerating monthly YoY growth. As a result, we remain cautious on the 
potential of exaggerated orders (including advance or duplicate orders) despite new 
orders intake continuing to outpace shipments, and we will continue to monitor the 
underlying end-demand closely.

### Page 4

Maintain Buy on AirTAC with a lower 12m TP of NT$1,895 (from NT$1,925). 
Investment Thesis - AirTAC 
AirTAC is the 2nd-largest pneumatic supplier in mainland China with 28% market share 
in 2025, outpacing most local competitors, owing to its low-cost yet high quality 
product, and service quality reputation in most key cities in mainland China. We forecast 
its market share will reach 30% in 2026E and expect AirTAC to be the structural winner 
in a deflationary market, due to its: (1) strong market growth and leadership: AirTAC 
continues to expand its market share in mainland China driven by competitive pricing 
that is more than 10% lower vs. international peers (with 45%+ GM / 30-35% OPM) and 
high demand elasticity among mainland China customers; and (2) superior cost structure 
and financial health: with best-in-class cash cost and a solid balance sheet, featuring 
positive net cash over FCF since 2023/2024, we believe AirTAC is well-equipped to 
weather industry downturns and capitalize on market restructuring. 
Overall, we believe AirTAC will see better revenue/profitability in the long term given its 
share gainer position in the China pneumatic market and potential to gain share in the 
linear guide market (improved demand in the next 1-2 years). This should lead to 
strengthened valuation, vs. its recent trading range (trading below its 10-year avg. P/E 
multiple). We are Buy rated on AirTAC. 
Price target risks and methodology - AirTAC 
Valuation methodology: Our 12m TP of NT$1,895 is based on a target P/E of 26.0x on 
our 2027E EPS (0.25x STDV above avg of the last pneumatic upcycle). 
Key downside risks: (1) Stronger competition from peers; (2) sharper-than-expected 
Rmb depreciation, and (3) a longer-than-expected end-market demand downtrend in 
mainland China.

Source: Company data, Goldman Sachs Research estimates, FactSet. Price as of 12 Oct 2026 close.

their respective areas of coverage.  The addition or removal of stocks from such Conviction Lists are managed by the Investment Review Committee or
other designated committee in each respective region and do not represent a change in the analysts’ investment rating for such stocks.

anticipated dividends, expected during the time horizon associated with the price target. Price targets are required for all covered stocks. The total
return potential, price target and associated time horizon are stated in each report adding or reiterating an Investment List membership.

ab 12 October 2026
Global Research
Largan Precision
Core weakness outweighs progress in AI optics
CPO progress is more tangible but substantial success is already priced in
We maintain Sell and our NT$5,000 target price as erosion in the core smartphone
business outweighs CPO progress. The Q326 call confirmed that Largan's fiber array has
passed customer qualification although its customer qualified another vendor's prism
micro-lens assembly (PMLA) that Largan would now buy in. If Largan qualifies the full

FAU plus carrier assembly for 2H27, content rises but with more pass-through cost and
less margin upside. We roll our valuation to 2028E, the first full year of CPO production,
though 25% below the NT$6,660 close on October 8.
Full FAU assembly would lift content, but with more pass-through
We believe Largan's FA customer is one of two  module suppliers on NVIDIA's 2H27
program, which we estimate could reach 40% share. The program is still subject to

successful yield ramp and could move to metalens in 2028, which would reopen supplier
qualification. Assembling the full FAU could lift Largan's ASP to US$125-150 leveraging
its precision alignment and automation but the PMLA, V-groove and carrier parts would
be pass through that cuts into margin leverage. We expand our FAU TAM analysis
(Figure 9S u m m a r y  o f  F A U  d r i v e r s :  1 2 . 3 m n / 3 5 . 6 m n  b a s e  c a s e  i n  2 0 2 8 - 2 0 3 0) with FAU market in 2028/30 expanding to 12.3mn/35.6mn units, with Largan

base case set at 25% of the TAM.
Core smartphone business stays soft into 2027
Q326 sales of NT$15.7bn, +15% QoQ/-11% YoY missedUBSe/Street +36%/+33% on
weaker flagship smartphone demand. GMs fell 7.2ppts QoQ to 42.2%, lowest in 13
years on renewable energy costs and low variable-aperture yields, dropping OpM to 17
year lows at 30.3% and EPS to NT$39.6, below UBS/street NT$51.50/NT$49.69. We cut
Q4 to NT$16.7bn +7% QoQ, 11% below consensus as its customer trimmed orders

after a pricier iPhone 18 launch and possible lag to Genius which is picking up periscope
share and outgrew in Q3 (+1% YoY vs. -11% YoY). We cut 2027 from NT$77bn to NT
$67bn, before a recovery to NT$79bn in 2028 with the next periscope upgrade. We see
GM recovering to 47.5%/48.9% in 2027-28 as yields improve and energy costs pass
through in 2028 pricing.
Maintain Sell and our NT$5,000 target
We cut 2026E/27E EPS by 14%/16% to NT$160/NT$180 and introduce 2028E EPS of

NT$220 for the core smartphone business. We value the lens business at NT$3,300 (15x
2028E ex-CPO EPS of NT$220, the middle of Largan's 13–18x historical range) and CPO
option at NT$1,700 or US$7bn (49x our base-case 2028E CPO EPS of NT$35, near the
top of optical peers' 14–54x). Our base case gives Largan 25% of a 12.3mn-unit, US
$1.85bn FAU market at US$150 per unit and a 50% gross margin. That credits it with
yields and margins it still needs to prove in a quarter when yields and energy costs cut

7.2ppts from its lens margin. At ~NT$6,660, the shares imply ~NT$69 of 2028E CPO
EPS, about twice our base case.

Core smartphone lens seeing weakness with declining

Q326 sales was reported light at NT$15.7bn, +15% QoQ missing UBSe/Street +36%/

orders after a pricier iPhone 18 launch and possible lag to Genius which is picking up
periscope share and outgrew in Q3 (flat vs. -11% YoY).  October revenue should be flat
MoM and November lower as the key customer has shortened its forecast after cutting
flagship orders in September. The spring-2027 model starts shipping in October but will

not offset the flagship decline.

CPO fiber array qualified, but rising pass-through as it upgrades to FAU
production
Largan's fiber array (FA) has passed qualification with a volume customer, and
management expects mass-production capacity by mid-2027 in a newly completed fab
dedicated to FA/FAU, using in-house equipment. The company's next step is to produce
the full detachable FAU mounted on a carrier, a board with three alignment beams that
sits on the optical engine's receptor. Largan can build FAUs now and says the customer

### Page 3

would prefer it to supply the full assembly, but qualification is pending with the
customer preferring FAU and carrier sampled separately.
Even as its potential to supply the full FAU improve, Largan will buy in most of the
content. The customer has qualified another PMLA vendor and prefers semiconductor-
made optics; V-grooves and carrier beams and boards are outsourced and the customer
does not want Largan to make MT ferrules. Management described its role as closer to a

contractor and said margins hinge on yields, as the FA carries a high ASP but also a large
bill of materials.  Supplying the complete FAU could raise billed content, but a larger
purchased bill of materials also dilutes the relationship between revenue and value
added. Our full-FAU ASP assumption requires Largan to capture that invoice scope.
Next year's product is grating-coupled and works for both CPO and NPO while edge-
coupled designs are being explored with the customer. The current product does not go

directly to end customers, but management expects to work directly with end customers
on next-generation designs (likely 2028) as it progresses toward multi-row FAs.
To ramp production, Largan has bought several land parcels and will build new fabs from
scratch, with construction finishing around 2030. It may outsource some capacity in the
interim and said it cannot yet be sure capacity will meet demand.
Annual FAU TAM reaches US$1.85bn in 2028 and US$4.5bn in 2030 in

our base case
We update our FAU (fiber array unit) TAM factoring CPO/NPO penetration into
AI networking switches, NVIDIA multi-rack systems, scale-up on compute and switch
boards, and non-NVIDIA accelerators.  We assume one passive FAU assembly connecting
a fiber array to a photonic engine with one unit per optical engine.
We assume one FAU per optical engine and 32 per switch ASIC in our base case,
matching NVIDIA's 32x3.2T engines on its 102.4T Spectrum-X package. Broadcom's

102.4Tb/s Davisson uses 16 engines at 6.4Tb/s, so our bear case assumes and even mix
of these two designs (24 per ASIC). We note that higher switch bandwidth does not
automatically increase FAU count if engine bandwidth rises at the same rate. Our 2030
assumption maintains the 32 FAUs per switch based on bandwidth rising to 204.8Tb/s
ASICs using 6.4Tb/s optical engines.

Our base case for the FAU market sums up 4 opportunities for CPO/NPO attach:
1. Scale-out and scale-across switches
We estimate annual switch ASIC shipments x CPO + NPO penetration x optical engines
per ASIC x FAUs per engine. In 2028, 25% CPO/NPO penetration or 1.0m 102.4T switch
ASICs at 32 FAUs each produces 8.0m FAUs. In 2030, 55% of 1.1mn 204.8T switch
ASICs at 32 FAUs drives a market size of 19.4mn FAUs.

2. NVIDIA's multi-rack connections for Rubin Ultra and Feynman
NVIDIA at its 2026 GTC San Jose proposed a multi-rack option to network together 8
Oberon NVL72 racks for the Rubin Ultra generation and 8 Kyber NVL144 racks.
Feedback at the time showed  possible transceiver or NPO connections for Rubin Ultra

and CPO connections for Feynman.  Our 2028 base case uses 110,000 racks, 15% multi-
rack adoption and 75% CPO/NPO penetration, giving approximately 12,400 connected
racks.
For 2030, our rack count falls to 35,000 racks as we assume Kyber would have 4x the
number of GPUs, equivalent to 140k Oberon equivalents. We assume the 35,000 racks
at 55% multi-rack adoption x 80% CPO/NPO attach gives 15,400 connected racks. We
assume about 216 and 576 inter-rack FAUs per rack, respectively, driving a market

opportunity of 2.7mn FAUs in 2028 and 8.9mn in 2030.

Overall market opportunity: 12.3mn/35.6mn generates a US$1.85bn/US
$4.5bn TAM in 2028/2030
We sum four pools: scale-out/scale-across switches, NVIDIA multi-rack pods, CPO native

NVIDIA GPUs and non-NVIDIA scale-up.  We add 10%/5% for FAUs lost at downstream
attach and test and for field spares, driving a market size of in our base case of 12.3mn
FAUs in 2028 and 35.6mn in 2030. At US$150/US$125 per FAU, that is a US$1.85bn
market in 2028 and US$4.5bn in 2030 (bear/bull US$0.5bn/US$3.5bn in 2028; US
$1.4bn/US$9.0bn in 2030). The expansion to 2030 comes from wider optical adoption
and greater content in scale-up systems, despite an assumed FAU ASP decline from US

$150 to US$125.

Scenario analysis on Largan's CPO opportunity
Our base case gives Largan the benefit of the doubt for successfully ramping up a full
FAU production and capturing 25% of the overall FAU TAM, yielding NT$35 of 2028E
EPS, about 16% of our 2028E lens EPS. A 25% share means 3.1mn FAUs in its first full
year of production: about 29% of NVIDIA-linked units and roughly three-quarters of the

allocation our checks suggest its module partner holds. We book these at the full US
$150 FAU price and 50% gross margin, allowing for an early-supply premium even
though the optics, V-grooves and carrier parts are bought in. That gives 2028E CPO sales
of US$462mn, rising to US$1.1bn and NT$84 of EPS in 2030 on a larger market.
Our bear case (15% share, 45% GM) gives NT$5 of CPO EPS in 2028E and NT$14 in
2030E; our bull case (35% share, 55% GM) gives NT$103 and NT$267. Each 5ppt of

share moves 2028E CPO EPS by ~NT$6.9 (~NT$340/share at 49x), and each 5ppt of
gross margin by ~NT$4.6 (~NT$225/share).  If gross margin were 35% rather than 50%,
holding operating expenses at 12% of sales, EPS would fall to approximately NT$21. A
next-generation shift in lens or coupling design could also change content and
alignment requirements including a potential metalens design which could invite in
different suppliers.
Our 2030 base case implies ~8.9mn Largan FAUs a year (21mn in the upside case),

though with risks around competition in a crowded field, yields and technology. A shift
to semiconductor-made optics or metalens would loosen alignment tolerances and
erode the precision premium behind Largan's pitch. The upside lies in direct work with
cloud providers on multi-row FAs from 2028, where Largan's multi-layer alignment is
most differentiated.

Net income (NT$mn) $589 $4,533 $13,453 $1,776 $10,925 $34,907
EPS (NT$) $5 $35 $103 $14 $84 $267
Shares 130.8 130.8 130.8 130.8 130.8 130.8
Source: UBS
Valuation and investment implications
We roll our valuation forward to 2028E and value Largan on a sum of the parts. We
value the lens business at NT$3,300 (15x 2028E EPS, the middle of Largan's 13–18x

historical range) and CPO at NT$1,700 (49x our base-case 2028E CPO EPS of NT$35,
near the top of optical peers' 14–54x). Our base case gives Largan 25% of a 12.3mn-
unit, US$1.85bn FAU market at US$150 per unit and a 50% gross margin. The 49x
multiple also assigns a substantial value to a business whose complete assembly still
requires qualification and whose margins still need to demonstrate can reach similar
levels to its mature smartphone business. At ~NT$6,660 recent share price level, the

shares imply ~NT$69 of 2028E CPO EPS, about twice our base case.
Upside risks include faster full-FAU qualification, firm volume commitments from
additional customers, stronger lens demand and better yields. Near-term catalysts are
the customer's capacity forecast and its visit to the new fab in the coming month. A
large forecast or faster FAU plus carrier qualification would be upside risks to our sell.

Forecast returns

Company Description
Founded in 1987, Largan Precision (Largan) is one of the world's leading manufacturers of
lenses used in cameras and IT products. It specialises in plastic/hybrid lenses and, to a lesser
extent, glass lenses and modules. In 2022, 20MP+ camera lenses were 10-20% of Largan's

shipment mix, 10MP lenses were 50-60%, 8MP lenses were 0-10% and other lenses were
30-40%.
Valuation Method and Risk Statement
Our price target is based on SOTP valuation on the CPO opportunity and traditional lens
business.
We think Largan's downside risks include: 1) ongoing pricing pressure from smartphone OEM
clients and increasingly intense competition among lens suppliers; and 2) a slower-than-
expected smartphone demand recovery in China, and 3) CPO commercial success below the

market's elevated expectations.
We believe the key upside risk is a faster-than-expected CPO qualification process at Largan.
In addition, if the company demonstrates exceptionally efficient and highly accurate
automation capabilities, it could support greater market share gains within the industry than
we currently anticipate.

Investing in structured investments involves significant risks. For a detailed discussion of the risks involved in investing in any particular structured investment, you must read

the relevant offering materials for that investment. Structured investments are unsecured obligations of a particular issuer with returns linked to the performance of an
underlying asset. Depending on the terms of the investment, investors could lose all or a substantial portion of their investment based on the performance of the underlying
asset. Investors could also lose their entire investment if the issuer becomes insolvent. UBS does not guarantee in any way the obligations or the financial condition of any

issuer or the accuracy of any financial information provided by any issuer. Structured investments are not traditional investments and investing in a structured investment is
not equivalent to investing directly in the underlying asset. Structured investments may have limited or no liquidity, and investors should be prepared to hold their investment
to maturity. The return of structured investments may be limited by a maximum gain, participation rate or other feature. Structured investments may include call features and,

if a structured investment is called early, investors would not earn any further return and may not be able to reinvest in similar investments with similar terms. Structured
investments include costs and fees which are generally embedded in the price of the investment. The tax treatment of a structured investment may be complex and may differ
from a direct investment in the underlying asset. UBS and its employees do not provide tax advice. Investors should consult their own tax advisor about their own tax situation

before investing in any securities.

would be consistent with its investment objective and other principal investment strategies. The returns on a portfolio incorporating ESG factors or sustainable investing
considerations may be lower or higher than portfolios where ESG factors, exclusions, or other sustainability issues are not considered by the portfolio manager, and the
investment opportunities available to such portfolios may differ.

## Document 140: 261016_daiwa_ Ardentec

Source PDF: all report pdf/261016_daiwa_ Ardentec.pdf
Report source: line報告備份
Date: 2026-10-16
Pages: 7
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Taiwan Information Technology

What’s new: We preview Ardentec’s 3Q26 results. In our initiation report, 
“Initiation: an emerging force in AI/HPC testing” (Sep 9), we expected 
traditional seasonality in 4Q26-1Q27 to weigh on Ardentec’s capacity 
utilisation. However, we now expect stronger-than-expected momentum in 
the networking segment to drive counter-seasonal resilience through 
1Q27E. We also expect AI revenue (with AI connectivity) contribution to 
exceed 40% in 1Q27E, well ahead of our original expectations.

What’s the impact: Robust networking demand bolsters high 
utilisation and CPO traction. Our industry checks indicate that demand 
for networking products is surging across the testing supply chain, primarily 
fuelled by the acceleration of 1.6T networking adoption. We forecast 
Ardentec’s revenue in 4Q26 and 1Q27 to beat typical seasonal slowdowns. 
As these high-speed networking programs largely follow a customer-
consignment model, their rising revenue mix should provide upside to 
overall gross margin. Concurrently, subsidiary Giga Solution is gaining 
momentum in co-packaged optics (CPO) testing, having already qualified 
and begun ramping up with leading US tier-1 players. While current 
engagement is centred on insertion 1, we see potential for customer base 
diversification and expansion into subsequent test insertions (2 and 3).

3Q26 results preview. With the Longtan fab expansion on track and 
spillover demand continuing, Ardentec 3Q26 revenue reached TWD5.34bn 
(+17.7% QoQ, +48.5% YoY), above the market consensus by 0.8% but 
slightly missing our estimate by 1.4%. With utilisation remaining high in 
3Q26, we estimate its gross margin and operating margin will reach 45.1% 
and 35.5%, respectively, above the consensus by 1.3pp and 1.9pp, 
respectively. We expect cleanroom construction of 2nd and 3rd floors will 
be finalised by year-end 2026 driven by solid domestic AI/HPC orders, with 
2nd-floor capacity likely contributing by late-1Q27.

What we recommend: We raise our 2027-28E EPS by 7% to reflect 
better-than-expected networking demand but lower our 2026E EPS by 1%. 
We reaffirm our Buy (1) rating and raise our 12M TP to TWD395 (from 
TWD340), based on an unchanged target PER of 25x on our 2027E EPS. 
While our target PER represents a premium over the OSAT/testing peer 
average, we believe this premium is justified by steady demand for high-
speed AI connectivity and the Longtan ramp-up. Our estimates indicate 
these factors could lift AI-related revenue to more than 40% of total sales 
by 1Q27. Key downside risk: delays in capacity expansion.

How we differ: Our 2027-28E EPS are 7-26% above the Bloomberg 
consensus, likely on our more aggressive utilisation rate and margin profile.

16 October 2026

3Q26 preview: AI defies seasonality

➢ Solid AI networking demand drives counter-seasonal resilience 
➢ On-track Longtan ramp and CPO upside expand advanced testing 
➢ Reaffirming our Buy (1) rating; raising our 12M TP to TWD395

Company profile 
Founded in 1999, Ardentec is a leading Taiwanese pure-play wafer-testing provider serving foundries, 
IDMs and fabless chipmakers. Its acquisitions of Giga Solution and Raytek over 2016-17 added RF 
testing and bumping capabilities. Combining automotive-grade reliability with flexible AI/HPC capacity, 
the company provides customers a secondary supplier which enhances supply-chain resilience.

ESG analysis

ESG risks
Risks Management Analyst comments
G
Executive/board
quality 2
The board demonstrates strong governance oversight, with 42% independent directors, regular
performance evaluations, and ESG-linked executive compensation. Governance quality is
further supported by a top 5% ranking in Taiwan’s corporate governance assessment for nine
consecutive years.
Capital management 2
Capital allocation appears disciplined, balancing expansion investments with shareholder
returns. The company funded major capacity growth while maintaining strong profitability and
distributed stable cash dividends, reflecting a prudent and shareholder-friendly capital
management approach.
Related party &
transaction 1
Related-party transactions appear well governed and transparent. The company states that
intercompany dealings follow arm’s-length principles and are not used for tax minimization, while
directors are required to disclose conflicts and abstain from related decisions.
Note: Management score represents a company's ability to manage/benefit from certain ESG topics. The scores range from 1 to 3, with 1 being the strongest.

旺矽 (6223 TT)  Buy

旺矽營收優於預期 ，CPO 設備出貨將帶動評價提升 ， 給Buy
的投資建議。  
投資建議 ：旺矽營收優於預期， CPO 設備出貨將帶動評價提升，
給 Buy 的投資建議。  
09/2026 營收優於群益預期 ：旺矽 09/2026 營收為 20.48 億元，
MoM-0.02% 、 YoY+82.23% ，累計營收為 150.68 億元，
YoY+58.02% 。實際月營收與群益預估的 16.50 億元，差異為
19.43% ，優於預期。  
3Q26 優於市場預期 ：市場先前預期旺矽 3Q26 營收 QoQ+0~5% ，
實際營收 QoQ+12.76% ，優於預期。  
旺矽有佈局 CPO 測試設備 ：旺矽的 Insertion 3 設備預計 4Q26
向晶圓代工 、IC 設計 、OSAT 送樣 ，1H27 有望進入量產 ；Insertion 
2 設備預計 2H26 會有較明顯的結果。  
旺矽積極擴產： 旺矽表示，目前在許多客戶端都是第一供應商，
供貨比重大多為 80% ，但之前因為產能供不應求，有外溢到第
二供應商，現在有積極擴產。  
旺矽預計 2027 年提高 PCB 的自製率： 探針卡可分為測試頭、
載板及 PCB 。旺矽表示，探針及載板均已全數自製， PCB 預計
2027 年開始量產，目標將 PCB 的自製率提升至 50% 。  
群益預估旺矽 2026 年 /2027 年稅後 EPS 64.86 元 /110.49 元： 群
益預估旺矽 2026 年 /2027 年營收為 214.11 億元 /363.85 億元，
YoY+60.13%/+69.94% ；稅後純益 63.57 億元 /108.28 億元，
YoY+100.11%/+70.35% ；稅後 EPS 64.86 元 /110.49 元。

2 群益投顧 
  ESG ：  
旺矽考慮營運對生態效益之影響，促進及宣導永續消費之概念，並依下列原
則從事研發、採購、生產、作業及服務等營運活動，以降低公司營運對自然
環境及人類之衝擊。  
旺矽應評估公司經營對社區之影響，並適當聘用公司營運所在地之人力，以
增進社區認同。  
旺矽經由股權投資、商業活動、捐贈、企業志工服務或其他公益專業服務等，
將資源投入透過商業模式解決社會或環境問題之組織，或參與社區發展及
社區教育之公民組織、慈善公益團體及政府機構之相關活動，以促進社區發
展。  
旺矽遵循上市上櫃公司治理實務守則、上市上櫃公司誠信經營守則及上市
上櫃公司訂定道德行為準則參考範例，建置有效之治理架構及相關道德標
準，以健全公司治理。

6 群益投顧 
  【投資評等說明】  
  評等  定義  
  強力買進 (Strong Buy)  首次評等潛在上漲空間 ≥ 35%  
  買進 (Buy)  15% ≤首次評等潛在上漲空間 <35%  
  區間操作 (Trading Buy) 5% ≤首次評等潛在上漲空間 <15%

中立 (Neutral)  
無法由基本面給予投資評等  
預期近期股價將處於盤整  
建議降低持股

【投資評等調整機制】  
  投資評等係「首次」給予特定個股投資評等時，其前一日收盤價相較 3 個月
目標價之潛在上漲空間計算而得。個股投資評等分為四個等級，定義如上。
爾後的投資評等係依循「首次評等」，直到停止推薦。   
「強力買進」、「買進」及「區間操作」均有 upside 目標價。差別僅在於，
首次評等時潛在報酬率不同。「中立」則無目標價。  
一旦我們給予特定個股「強力買進」 、 「買進」或「區間操作」之投資評等，
就是責任的開始，爾後將透過各式研究報告作定期性、持續性追蹤基本面及
股價變化，直到停止推薦。  
停止推薦情境：  
1.  達目標價。  
2.  雖未達原訂目標價，但檢視基本面、訊息面、籌碼面等多方訊息，研
判股價上漲空間已然有限，將適時出具降評報告。  
3.  推薦後股價不漲反跌。

【免責聲明】  
  本研究報告僅提供予特定人之客戶作為參考資料「非經同意不得轉載」 。我
們並不確保此資訊的完整性與正確性，投資人應了解，報告中有關未來預測
之陳述可能不會實現，因而不應被依賴。而且此報告並非根據特定投資目的
或依預定對象之財務狀況所撰寫出來的，因此，此研究報告的目的，既非對
投資人於買賣證券、選擇權、期貨或其他證券相關之衍生性商品提供詢價服
務，亦非作為進行交易的要約。投資人應注意到相關證券之價值及收益，可
能會有無預警地上升或下降，產生投資回報金額可能比原始投資來得少的
情形。

3Q26Re: 신규 시설 매출 인식 개시 
연결 기준 매출액 1조 4,903억원(+18.5%YoY, 이하 YoY 생략), 영업이익 6,401억원
(+1.0%, OPM 42.9%)로 컨센서스를 상회하는 실적 기록. 1~4공장이 완전 가동을 유지하는 
한편, 5공장은 PPQ 배치 생산 본격화되면서 매출 기여 확대. 상업 생산 전환이 예상되는 
2H27부터 이익 기여 커질 것으로 기대. 
연초 인수한 Rockville 공장은 3Q에도 안정적인 상업 생산을 이어갔으며 2Q 생산 물량의 
매출이 인식되며 매출 성장에 기여. 동사는 Rockville 공장 매출 인식 등을 반영하여 기존 
+15~20% 성장에서 +20%로 연간 가이던스 상향.   
고객사 수주 회복 기대 
3Q26 기준 누적 수주액은 219억 달러로 전분기 대비 소폭 증가했으나 과거 연간 수주액 
증가 대비 부족한 수준. 저조한 수주 실적은 지난해 초 의약품에 대한 관세 불확실성 증가로 
고객사들의 수주 문의가 저조했던 영향. 26년 초부터 관세 불확실성이 낮아지며 수주 문의
는 회복 추세. 약 1년 정도 논의 기간이 소요되는 것을 고려할 때 26년말~27년 초 수주 회
복 확인 가능할 것으로 기대. 
안정적인 공급망 유지가 중요한 CDMO 산업 특성상 노조 파업 가능성도 고객사 수주를 늦
추는 요인으로 판단. 다만, 10/16 인천 고등법원이 쟁의행위 가처분 항고심에 대해 인용 결
정을 하며 기존 3개 공정에서 9개 공정으로 확대되며 파업 우려는 다소 완화. 
투자 의견 Buy, 목표 주가 200만원 유지 
매수 의견과 목표 주가 200만원을 유지. 원달러 약세에도 불구하고 5공장 Ramp-up과 
Rockville 공장 매출 기여로 견조한 실적 성장 지속될 것으로 예상. 파업 이슈 해소와 대형 
수주 계약이 주가 상승을 이끌 것으로 판단.

## Document 258: Allianz_2026-10-16_Invest in Germany, Yes, But How Turning the investment gap into Europe’s biggest opportunity_00e2290c

Source PDF: all report pdf/Allianz_2026-10-16_Invest in Germany, Yes, But How Turning the investment gap into Europe’s biggest opportunity_00e2290c.pdf
Report source: Allianz Trade
Date: 2026-10-16
Pages: 14
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1

In Summary  
• 
 The first 
 Invest in Germany  
Summit  
takes place in Berlin on 19 and 20 October 2026, 
asking why and how Germany can attract 
 investment. The “why”: a top
 -
tier location 
that chronically underinvests, including from abroad.  
 Germany is the world’s third
 -
largest economy (~EUR4.4trn GDP), the only G7 country with a AAA rating from all three 
major agencies, and the third most attractive investment destination in Europe in 
 a 
2026 
EY survey. It has 1,700 hidden champions in the Mittelstand  
and R&D at 3.1% of GDP. 
Foreign companies are already here  
with 
an  
inward FDI stock 
 exceed
 ing  
EUR
 1.
0
tn
. Yet 
between 2019 and 2025, total 
 gross fixed 
 investment in Germany fell 6.4%, while it rose 
6.7% in the rest of the eurozone and 20.2% in the U.S. The gap is around EUR390bn versus 
Europe and up to 
 EUR730bn versus the U.S. Foreign investors are not closing it: 
announced 
 greenfield 
 investment 
 projects fell 10% in 2025 to 548 and are 44% below 
2019, the lowest since 2009. German savers face the same problem. Households and 
corporations save more than they invest, 
 and the surplus goes abroad (net foreign assets 
of 87% of GDP) at returns about 5pp below the U.S. and close to 3pp below other 
European countries since 1975, even below domestic returns. If German savers earn more 
at home, so can international investors.  
• 
 The problem is not a lack of capital but a lack of sufficiently attractive opportunities 
to deploy it, for domestic and foreign investors alike.  
Net public investment averaged 
zero between 2002 and 2015. Years of underinvestment should have raised returns on 
new capital, but high energy and labor costs (compounded by the second
 -
highest OECD 
tax wedge), bureaucracy (up to EUR146bn of lost output pe
 r year, per ifo
 ) and skills 
shortages prevent it. Investors themselves point to Germany’s integration of industry, 
science and research, legal certainty and skilled workforce as strengths, and to energy 
costs as Europe’s main disadvantage as an investment location
 . 
Financing is also a weak 
point: corporate financing costs are higher than in France or Italy, partly due to a 
fragmented banking sector, and projects are often too small for institutional investors 
(11,000 local authorities, 800 municipal utilities).  
• 
 The equation is changing: the fiscal shift kick
 -
starts investment, and reforms and 
public risk
 -
sharing open the door to private capital, German and international.  
The 
debt
 -
brake reform lifts the deficit from 2
 -
3% to 4
-
5% of GDP until 2028, funding defense 
and infrastructure. The “Deutschlandfonds
 ” (EUR30bn of public guarantees and equity) 
aims to mobilize EUR130bn of private capital by covering risks that private investors will 
not take alone. Foreign capital is already responding: FDI inflows rose about 50% to 
EUR86bn in 2025
 . 
European sources 
 contributed more than 
 80% of the total and the UK 
was 
 the largest single investor at 31%. Domestic savings 
 could 
 add to the pool: shifting 
5% of household financial assets out of deposits would mobilize ~EUR450bn, repatriating 
10% of net foreign assets ~EUR400bn. The “Altersvorsorgedepot” (from 1 January 2027, 
~EUR150bn over five years) and the proposed “Kapitalrente
 ” (a public fund of 
~EUR560bn by 2040) would build a domestic institutional investor base, a natural co
 -
investor for international capital. But much of the Altersvorsorgedepot may flow into 
global funds unless Germany becomes more attractive.

Invest in Germany, Yes, But How?  
Turning the investment gap into Europe’s biggest opportunity

### Page 2

2

• 
 Germany’s investment gap can therefore become one of Europe’s largest investment 
opportunities, in four verticals: energy, digital infrastructure, transport and mobility, 
and defense.  
In energy, needs reach ~EUR1.3trn by 2045 (about 30% of 2025 GDP) for 
renewables, grids, storage and hydrogen. Projects are often too small for institutional 
investors (11,000 local authorities, 800 Stadtwerke), so the enablers are advisory 
support, stand
 ardized tenders and aggregation at district and county level, and low
 -
carbon FDI rose 25% across Europe in 2025. Digital infrastructure needs EUR275
 -
500bn 
by 2030 (6
 -
11% of GDP) for hardware, telecoms, software and data centers, and foreign 
capital is alre
 ady committing, for example Google, Microsoft and Nvidia. The constraint 
is grid connection and permitting, not capital, so connection approvals should be 
prioritized. Transport and mobility 
 has  
EUR166bn planned for 2025
 -
29 (EUR107bn rail, 
EUR52bn federal roads, EUR8bn waterways), with PPP 2.0 as the enabler: performance
 -
linked service
 -
level agreements and long
 -
term offtake contracts that tie returns to 
predictable cash flows. Defense financing i
 s ~EUR734bn over five years, enough to meet 
NATO’s 3.5% of GDP target for core defense by 2029, and defense FDI projects rose 84% 
across Europe in 2025, with simpler regulation and procurement as the enabler. Across 
all four, the common enablers are faster  
 permitting and procurement, grid access, 
standardized structures and performance
 -
based PPPs with long
 -
term offtake contracts. 
Success would lift potential growth and ease pressure on the social state. Failure would 
leave Germany with higher debt and littl
 e improvement in long
 -
term growth.

Germany’s chronic investment shortage despite appealing conditions  
Germany's recent economic weakness should not obscure its enduring strengths and in
 -
principle attractiveness 
for investment.  
After several years of stagnation, the country remains Europe's largest economy, one of the world's 
leading industrial and exporting nations and home to globally competitive companies, world
 -
class research 
institutions, deep pools of skilled labor and exc
 eptionally strong public institutions. For long
 -
term investors, 
Germany offers a rare combination of economic scale, technological capability, political stability and financial 
strength.  
• 
 Scale matters: Germany is the EU's largest and the world's third
 -
largest economy (~EUR4.4trn GDP) 
meaning every investment project offers access to a substantial domestic market.  
Germany is also the 
third largest exporter, with one of the world's deepest manufacturing ecosystems, strong in machinery, 
chemicals, electrical equipment, vehicles pharmaceuticals, industrial automation and precision 
engineering, carried by a broad netwo
 rk of medium
 -
sized specialist firms. The 2026 EY attractiveness 
survey ranks Germany as the third most attractive investment destination in Europe, with a narrowing gap 
towards the top spots (France and UK).  
• 
 Its AAA rating and top institutional quality provide a stable framework for investors.  
Germany is the only 
G7 country that enjoys a AAA rating from the top three agencies, indicative of comfortably solid public 
finances. It has the top Rule of Law score in the G20 and ranks among the top 20 out of 208 countries in the 
World Bank’s various G
 overnance Indicators, reflecting strong rule of law, effective institutions, democratic 
accountability and relatively low corruption. This institutional strength provides a predictable and stable 
framework for businesses.  
• 
 The “Mittelstand
 :” 1700 hidden champions beyond the 40 global DAX companies form a uniquely strong 
economic ecosystem.  
Around 25% of German exports originate from medium
 -
sized companies that are 
niche B2B world market leaders. These hidden champions are strong innovators, filing patents at ~5x the 
rate of large corporates per employee. Many are privately held and often fa
 mily
-
owned.  
• 
 Germany is an innovation powerhouse and automation leader.  
Ranked 11th out of 139 economies in the 
2025 WIPO Global Innovation Index, it hosts seven of the world's leading innovation clusters and performs 
strongly in science, patent generation and logistics, where it ranks third globally. It is the world's fourth  
most 
automated manufacturing economy, with 449 industrial robots per 10,000 manufacturing workers (vs. a 
global average of 132). Its commitment to innovation is reflected in R&D spending equivalent to 3.1% of 
GDP, placing it 10th among roughly 80 countrie
 s tracked and ahead of the OECD average of 2.7%. Foreign

### Page 3

3

investors see this similarly: they name the close integration of industry, science and research, legal certainty 
and the skilled workforce as positives of Germany.
 1 
• 
 Human capital and scientific excellence.  
German nationals have won 17 Nobel prizes in scientific fields in 
the last 30 years alone, most recently this year (for medicine on 5 October). Max Planck Society researchers 
have been involved in 31 Nobel prizes since the institution’s founding
 . Three German universities are listed 
in the global top 50 on two different major international rankings: the Technical University of Munich, LMU 
Munich, which scored a perfect 100% on industry connections, and the University of Heidelberg. The country 
al
 so has the highest share of STEM graduates in the OECD.  
• 
 Quality of life and attractiveness for global talent.  
Three German cities make the global top 10 in Mercer’s 
most recent Quality of Living ranking (Frankfurt, Munich and Duesseldorf). Moreover, Germany is not only 
among the frontrunners in tackling climate change but also ranks sixth in Yale’s Environmental 
Performance Index, reinforcing its appeal as a destination for international talent.  
Despite these structural advantages, a massive investment gap has opened. 
 A key macroeconomic fact about 
Germany is its chronic lack of investment, which holds back capital accumulation, growth, and productivity. 
Compared to the U.S., the investment gap is very large (Figure 1): while U.S. fixed investment has increased by a 
factor of 2  
compared to the beginning of the millennium, Germany’s has increased only by 
 20%
. Even compared to 
its EU peers such as France, Sweden, or even the eurozone average, however, German investment lags.  
What’s more, German investment has declined outright since the pandemic. 
 Between 2019 and 2025, aggregate 
investments in Germany declined by 
 -
6.4%, compared with +6.7% in the rest of the Eurozone and +20.2% in the U.S. 
which alone leaves an investment gap of around EUR390bn versus Europe or EUR730bn vs the U.S. Foreign 
investor
s are not closing this gap. 
 T
he number of foreign investment projects announced in Germany fell by 10% in 
2025 to 548, the lowest level since 2009.
 2 
The decline was smaller than in France (
 -
17%) and the UK (
 -
14%
) but  
compared with 2019 Germany has lost more: 
 -
44%, against 
 -
28% in France and 
 -
34% in the UK (Figure 2). Unlike in 
France and the UK, the number has fallen every year since 2019.  
Foreign companies are nevertheless well represented in Germany. The stock of inward FDI stood at 
 EUR
 1.
0
tn at the 
end of 2024.
 3 
The German investment promotion agency GTAI recorded 1,564 foreign investment projects in 2025, 
9.3% fewer than in 2024 but a smaller decline than in the EU (
 -
18.1%) and worldwide (
 -
9.5%).
4

underperformance especially since 2019.

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research

The composition of investment is skewed away from productivity
 -
enhancing items.  
Germany is a laggard in capex 
related to information and communication technology (ICT 
 – 
Figure 3), with ICT
 -
related investment less than half 
the OECD average as a share of GDP. These are investment items that are crucial determinants of productivity 
grow
 th.

Figure 3: ICT investment by asset (2024, in % of GDP): Germany ranks low compared to peers

Sources: OECD, LSEG Datastream, own calculations, Allianz Research  
External shocks don’t explain investment underperformance. 
 External shocks 
 – 
 such as the 2007
 -
08 financial 
crisis, Covid
 -
19, Russia’s full
 -
scale invasion of Ukraine, or the Iran war 
 – 
no doubt played a role. Energy price shocks 
have suppressed investment in Germany’s energy
 -
intensive industrial sectors. However, g
 iven that Germany’s 
investment underperformance is chronic, and that its peers were subject to the same shocks, these shocks only offer 
a partial explanation for Germany’s lack of investment.

### Page 5

5

Figure 4: Gross investment versus gross saving in % of GDP (2024): Germany saves but does not invest.

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Bird’s eye view: plenty of saving, not enough investment. 
 Germany’s lack of investment is particularly noteworthy 
given the fact that the economy generates large saving flows (Figure 4). National income accounting identities tell 
us that the gap between saving (S) and investment (I) is an economy’s current accoun
 t (CA) balance: CA = S 
 – 
I or, 
when abstracting from income flows on foreign assets, its export surplus.
 5  
Because the identity holds for the economy as a whole and for each sector, we can trace the surplus to its origins by 
looking at the saving
 -
investment balance of households, government, and financial and non
 -
financial corporations 
(NFC), which together add  
up to the current account (Figure 5). It turns out that, in addition to households’ persistent 
surplus of saving over investment, NFC have also been exhibiting saving surpluses. The government now invests 
more than it saves, after having made positive con
 tributions to the CA balance over much of the 2010s. In turn, CA 
surpluses imply capital exports: the excess of saving over investment is simply invested abroad.  
Figure 5: Saving net of investment by sector, and current account balance (% of GDP): Households have not been 
the only savers

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research

5 
Ignoring primary income on net foreign assets and international transfers, the national income identity can be written as Y =  
C + G + I + NX, 
that is, GDP equals the sum of consumption, government spending, investment, and net exports. This can be rearran
 ged to give net exports as 
the difference between saving and investment NX = Y 
 – 
(C + G) 
 – 
I = S 
– 
I since national saving is the excess of GDP over domestic demand from 
consumption and government spending S = Y 
 – 
(C + G). In fact, Germany’s primary income  
balance (income earned on net foreign assets) is 
now almost as large as net exports
 . 
See Bundesbank (2026), Germany’s balance of payments in 2025, Monthly Report, March 2026.

### Page 6

6

Large foreign assets, poor returns  
Germany’s net foreign assets are 87% of GDP
 . Germany has amassed a large amount of claims against the rest of 
the world: its net international investment position (NIIP) 
 – 
 the difference between foreign assets and foreign 
liabilities 
 – 
is about 87% of GDP (Figure 6). From a macroeconomic perspectiv
 e, capital exports can in principle be 
beneficial for a country if it earns higher returns abroad than at home: the proof of the pudding is in the eating.  
Figure 6: Germany’s Net International Investment Position (USD bn): Savings go abroad.

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Returns on foreign assets: low 
 – 
and inferior to domestic returns
 . Germany has earned a poor return on its foreign 
assets for decades. Since 1975, its average annual foreign returns were about 5 percentage points below those of 
the U.S. and close to 3 points below those of other European countries (Table 1). What’s more
 , Germany’s foreign 
asset returns were considerably lower than the returns on its domestic assets: domestic saving has been 
systematically directed to foreign assets that earn less than the investment opportunities available at home.
 6 
Table 1: Returns on foreign assets 1975-2020 by country: Germany showing subdued returns on its foreign 
investments.

Source: Hünnekes et al. (2025). 
This suggests that Germany’s current account surplus 
 – 
or its export surplus 
 – 
turns out not to be a “good deal.” 
Instead, more investment at home would be optimal from a macroeconomic perspective. But it also begs the 
question: what are the reasons for Ge
 rmany’s excess saving?

6

### Page 7

7

The 
 multiple 
 origins of chronic investment shortage  
Households are 
 risk
 -
averse.  
 Germany’s high level of saving is usually attributed to prudent, highly risk
 -
averse 
households. Indeed, German households’ saving rate out of disposable income (Figure 7) is high in international 
comparison. At the same time, it is “natural” for the house
 hold sector in aggregate to feature a surplus of saving 
over investment. Households face an uncertain income stream but wish to smooth consumption. Consequently, they 
spread resources over a lifetime through saving  
making household surpluses a common feature of most economies.

Figure 7: Household gross saving in % of disposable income (2025): German households are heavy savers.

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Together with other factors such as risk aversion, the lack of a “Kapitalmarktkultur” (“capital markets culture”) and 
financial literacy
 7
, the institutional setup of pensions provision affects household saving incentives substantially: The 
public pay
 -
as
 -
you
-
go pension system reduces the need for households to bear market risk for retirement provision. 
As a result, German households hold 37%  
of their financial assets in bank deposits (Figure 8).  
Figure 8: Asset classes as % of total gross financial assets: Bank deposits still a key saving vehicle

Sources: Eurostat, national central banks, financial supervisory authorities, financial associations and statistical 
offices, IMF, LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research. 
In turn, banks intermediate those savings in bank deposits into low
 -
risk, low
 -
return assets: corporate loans, which 
favor incumbent firms (due to established relationships) rather than new, innovative firms; and government bonds 
(due to favorable regulator
 y treatment of banks’ holdings). As a result, savings contribute little to the creation of 
risk
-
bearing capital, and available funds for startups and scaling is scarce.

7 
Allianz Financial Literacy Report 2026
 , Financial Literacy Pays: Smarter Investing 
 Goes Beyond AI
 .

### Page 8

8

Figure 5 suggests that, in addition to households, two more sectors have in recent years tended to have a surplus of 
saving over investment: the general government 
 (
federal, state, and local government together with social security 
funds
 )
, and non
 -
financial corporations.  
The lack of government investment contributed to a lack of demand and poor infrastructure. 
 Government 
surpluses of saving over investment partly reflected a lack of public investment. Between 2002 and 2015 net 
government investment (gross investment less depreciation on the public capital stock) was zero, on average 
(Figure 9). The government ba
 rely invested enough to keep the public capital stock from shrinking.  
Only since 2015 gross investment has been sufficient to keep net investment positive. At 3.5% of GDP in 2025, 
government gross investment  
is now catching up with peers. As a result of this lack of government investment, 
Germany’s infrastructure has deteriorated, which is likely to have contributed to the lack of domestic investment by 
firms.
8  
Figure 9: General government gross vs net investment in % of GDP: Improvement underway

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Corporate surpluses persist.  
Figure 5 shows that the non
 -
financial corporate (NFC) sector has exhibited a surplus 
of saving over investment since around the turn of the millennium. Corporate saving is  
simply retained earnings, i.e. 
profits kept in the firm after paying interest, taxes and dividends. These surpluses became substantial after the great 
financial crisis and have persisted since at macro
 -
significant levels (on average since 2010: 1.6 % of G
 DP; 2025 still 
1.1% of GDP). While an excess of saving over investment is “natural” for households, it is not necessarily the case for 
NFCs. Firms invest to earn  
returns and will usually borrow to invest (or issue equity). Figure 10 shows the persistent 
shortfall of NFC investment vs saving.  
Figure 10: German non-financial corporations, gross saving vs gross investment, % of GDP: The gap persists

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research

8 
In its 2026 survey, German chamber of commerce (DIHK) 
 members cited poor infrastructure as one of the reasons for investing 
 abroad
 . 
DIHK
 -
Konjunkturumfrage 2026
 , Sonderauswertung Auslandsinvestitionen
 .

### Page 9

9

There are several reasons for the NFC surplus.  
• 
 Wage moderation and strong external demand
 . Real wages in the NFC sector have lagged productivity 
for nearly two decades (Figure 11). Only recently has real compensation for employees caught up. All else 
equal, wages lagging productivity would have boosted firms’ profits. Strong external demand, i
 f not 
accompanied by a commensurate increase in investment spending, will do the same. As shown in Figure 
1
0
, gross NFC saving jumped during periods of strong global demand (2009
 -
11 and after the pandemic, 
2020
 -
22). 
• 
 Lack of domestic demand
 . Wage moderation and a lack of public investment contributed to sluggish 
domestic demand, in turn suppressing economic growth. One consequence was that it made investment in 
faster
 -
growing overseas markets more attractive.  
• 
 Tax incentives favoring retained earnings.  
German corporate tax reforms (2001, 2008) made retaining 
profits more attractive than paying dividends, lowering payout rates and raising NFC gross saving.
 9 
These 
incentives matter in Germany because a large share of corporate profits are generated by privately held 
firms. In privately held firms, owners’ personal tax incentives, rather than pure profit maximization, may 
shape payout decisions. As the additio
 nal saving was not absorbed by investment (Figure 10), it resulted in 
higher net lending.  
Figure 11: Real compensation vs real value added per hour in the non -financial corporate sector (index, 2000 = 
100): wage restraint has contributed to non-financial sector surpluses until recently

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Economic theory suggests that years of underinvestment should raise expected returns and attract capital. In 
Germany, however, a range of supply
 -
side constraints is preventing this adjustment from taking place. 
 Energy 
costs remain elevated following the loss of cheap Russian pipeline gas and the shift towards more expensive LNG 
imports, compounded by high taxes and network charges. Businesses surveyed by EY in 2026 also see the cost of 
energy as Europe’s main dis
 advantage as an investment location, although 60% of them expect Europe’s 
attractiveness to improve over the next three years.
 10 
Labor costs are also burdened by Germany's exceptionally 
high tax wedge, the second highest in the OECD. Bureaucracy further weighs on competitiveness. According to the 
ifo economic research institute, excessive administrative requirements reduce annual o
 utput by up to EUR 146bn, 
or roughly 4% of GDP.  
11 
Although Germany ranks 12th out of 45 countries for overall business attractiveness, it ranks 
second
 -
to
-
last on costs.  
12 
Other structural weaknesses are equally apparent. Demographic decline is reducing the 
supply of skilled labor, while transport, logistics and digital infrastructure bear the consequences of years of 
underinvestment. Financing conditions are less favorable  
than in France or Italy, despite lower German risk
 -
free 
rates, with the fragmented banking system likely playing a role. These long
 -
standing challenges are now being 
compounded by structural shocks. Competition from China has intensified to the point that  
Germany runs a negative 
trade balance with China even in capital goods, for decades its core strength. Meanwhile, parts of the automotive 
sector misjudged both technology and market positioning, favoring combustion engines and fuel cell technology

9 
See 
 Dao, Mai Chi (202
 0
): Wealth Inequality and Private Savings: The Case of Germany.  
IMF 
Working Paper 
 20/107
 . 
10 
EY (2026), European Attractiveness Survey, June 2026 (500 executives surveyed between February and March 2026). 
 The results refer to 
Europe as a whole.

Bürokratie in Deutschland kostet jährlich 146 Milliarden Euro an Wirtschaftsleistung | Pressemitteilung | ifo Institut

EconStor: Wirken die Maßnahmen der neuen Bundesregierung auf Investitionen in Deutschland? 
 Ergebnisse einer Unternehmensbefragung

over affordable electric vehicles. As a result, the higher returns that theory predicts after prolonged underinvestment 
are being eroded by a combination of cost pressures, institutional inefficiencies and structural headwinds.

What needs to be done: Germany’s fiscal push is a first step, but private capital needs to 
follow  
Germany’s fiscal push heralds potential investment turnaround. 
 Yet at least some of the demand
 -
side reasons for 
Germany’s large surpluses of saving over investment are on the mend. The German government has put in place a 
large fiscal expansion centered on infrastructure capex and defense. 
 Following the reform of its debt brake in 2025, 
public deficits are expected to rise from around 2
 -
3% to 4
-
5% of GDP in the next years, funding hundreds of billions 
of euros in additional spending on infrastructure, defense, energy, climate mitigation & ad
 aption and digitalization
 . 
The government’s fiscal package thus promises to kill two birds with one stone when it comes to improving German 
investment perspectives. In the short run it boosts investment spending, while in the longer run it improves public 
infrastructure, spurring 
 private investment demand.  
Government money alone is not enough: attracting private capital is necessary.  
Closing Germany’s investment 
gap will require more than public funding. The scale of the country’s investment needs is such that substantial 
private capital must also be mobilized. Foreign capital is already responding to some extent. 
 B
ased on Bundesbank 
data, FDI inflows rose by about 50% to around EUR86bn in 2025, after a fall of almost 32% in 2024  
(Figure 1
 2
)
.
13 
Investors from the UK raised their investment by 284% to EUR26bn and are now the largest single source (31% of 
the total), while U.S. investment fell by 44% to EUR11.8bn. Other EU countries contributed EUR43bn, so European 
investors account for more than 
 80% of the total.  
Figure 12: Foreign direct investment inflows to Germany, EUR bn and % of GDP: Rebound in the making

Sources: LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research 
Germany's challenge is not a shortage of capital but rather directing this capital towards productive investment.  
German households hold around EUR9trn in financial assets, a large proportion of which, by international 
standards, sits in cash and bank deposits (EUR3.4trn).
 14
. Meanwhile, the country's net foreign asset position exceeds 
EUR4.0trn (87% of GDP).  Unlocking even a small portion of these savings could have a transformative impact. 
Shifting just 5% of German households' financial assets out of deposits into more pro
 ductive assets would mobilize 
roughly EUR450bn of investment capital. In addition, repatriating for example 10% of the country’s net foreign 
investments would translate to EUR400bn of additional domestic capital.  
Making Germany more attractive for private investment starts with raising expected returns.  
 Years of 
underinvestment have already increased the potential returns on capital, as scarcity raises the marginal product of 
capital. But unlocking this opportunity requires reducing regulatory barriers, expanding standardized investment 
structures and st
 rengthening the investment culture through tax incentives, pension and insurance reforms, public
 -
private partnerships standards and strategic investment funds. Bottlenecks such as permitting, procurement, deal 
structuring and regulatory friction must be re
 duced. A key enabler for all relevant investment opportunities is

13

(2026),

 . 
14 P
ension funds and life insurance products account for EUR2.5trn and securities for EUR3.1trn.

11

simplification: permitting and procurement procedures need to be accelerated and harmonized to reduce lead 
times. The rapid build
 -
out of LNG terminals following the 2022 energy crisis proved that Germany is capable of 
quick infrastructure investments.  
The newly set up “Deutschlandfonds,” with its EUR30bn guarantees and equity targeting to mobilize EUR130bn 
in private capital, is an important step in the right direction  
as it directly targets the gap between Germany's 
capital
 -
willing private investors and the risk they won't take on without a public backstop. It covers guarantees for 
large
 -
scale capex in key sectors (power generation, hydrogen, automotive supply chains);  
a raw materials fund 
securing critical minerals through extraction, processing, and recycling and expanded venture financing for start
 -
ups, scale
 -
ups, deep tech, AI, biotech and defense.   
“Water, water, everywhere, nor any drop to drink”: Germany’s intermediation problem.  
The issue of available 
saving versus domestic investment 
 – 
or the country’s current account surplus 
 – 
is in large part about 
 where  
Germany 
invests. A related, but separate, question is how the savings that stay domestic are used. It turns out that another 
factor holding back investment in Germany is the intermediation of existing savings. Germany, like many other EU 
countries, is par
 ticularly lacking in long
 -
term capital due to the underdevelopment of pension funds (Figure 1
 3
), 
since public pay
 -
as
 -
you
-
go (PAYG) systems dominate retirement income provision.
 15 
Key reform: towards a more funded retirement system
 . Scandinavia and the Netherlands, economies with similar 
“culture” to Germany, have much higher stock market capitalization as a share of GDP not least because households 
accumulate claims on private pensions, which in turn recycle savings into the domest
 ic equity market (Figure 1
 4
).
16 
The development of pension funds is the key reform because they are upstream of venture capital and private 
equity in the financing chain.  
Figure 13: Insurance and pensions assets as % of nominal GDP, 2025: underdeveloped in Germany

Sources: Eurostat, national central banks, financial supervisory authorities, financial associations and statistical 
offices, IMF, LSEG Datastream, Allianz Research. 
The pension commission’s proposal for a funded “Kapitalrente” financed by an additional 2% contribution 
starting 
 from 2028 and invested in a public fund alongside private alternatives, would be a major step in the 
right direction
 .
17 
It would add a capital
 -
funded component to the pay
 -
as
 -
you
-
go system in pillar 1 and channel 
household retirement saving into equity markets at scale. According to estimates, the public fund could reach 
around EUR560bn by 2040 (8% of GDP) and become one of  
the world’s largest pension funds within two decades.
 18 
This would create a domestic institutional investor base that sits upstream from venture capital and private equity.  
As an additional pillar 3 contribution to Germany's pension system, the forthcoming “Altersvorsorgedepot” can 
also strengthen domestic investment.  
This reform, which has already been passed, will replace the Riester pension 
from 1 January 2027. Savers will be able to invest in shares, funds and ETFs without a capital guarantee
 . 
The state 
will pay up to 50 cents for every euro saved, up to a total of 360 euros, and an additional 25 cents for every euro 
saved up to a total of 1,800 euros. This results in a maximum annual subsidy of 540 euros.

How to invest in Germany: focus on key investment themes and incentives  
From underinvestment to investment opportunity 
 – 
why now is the right time to close Germany’s up to EUR730bn 
investment gap.  
Germany's investment gap presents both a problem and an opportunity. Years of underinvestment 
have created enormous modernization needs and, in principle, high marginal returns to new capital. Until now, 
structural bottlenecks prevented investors from cap
 turing those returns. The combination of fiscal expansion, reform 
and public
 -
private risk sharing could now begin to change that equation.  The key question that arises next is where 
and how to invest. The answer can be found in four key areas: energy, dig
 ital infrastructure, transport and mobility 
and defense.  
Energy transition:  
The move towards net
 -
zero emissions and greater geopolitical resilience will require substantial 
investment in renewable energy, electricity grids, storage solutions and hydrogen infrastructure. Germany will 
require around EUR1.3tn in investments for rene
 wables, grid infrastructure, storage and hydrogen by 2045, which 
is equivalent to 30% of GDP in 2025.  Financing vehicles and project pipelines are key enablers. However, with 
around 11,000 local authorities and 800 municipal utilities (“Stadtwerke”), indi
 vidual projects are often too small to 
attract institutional investors. To address this, municipalities and regions should be supported through advisory 
services, standardized tender frameworks and training programs to facilitate the structuring of investa
 ble public
 -
private partnerships. Greater participation by districts (“
 Regierungsbezirke”) and counties (“Kreise”) would facilitate 
project aggregation and create scalable investment pipelines. Foreign investors are showing interest here: 
according to EY, the number of low
 -
carbon energy FDI projects in Europe rose by 25% in 2
 025.
21 
Digital infrastructure:  
 Digitalization and digital infrastructure are critical drivers of productivity growth. It is 
estimated that a cumulative investment of between EUR275bn and 500bn will be required by 2030, equivalent to 
6
–
11% of GDP in 2025. This includes spending on compu
 ter hardware, telecommunications equipment, software 
and AI
 -
related infrastructure, such as data centers. Grid access and permitting reform are key enablers. Germany 
should therefore prioritize grid
 -
connection approvals and permitting procedures for data c
 enters and digital 
infrastructure, treating computing capacity with the same urgency as energy security. Multi
 -
year connection queues 
have become the primary constraint on Germany’s AI and digital expansion, rather than a lack of capital or demand. 
Foreign  
capital is already 
 committing: Google plans to invest EUR5.5bn in Germany until 2029, Microsoft announced

19 
Indefi (2026): The German Pension Reform and the Shape of Things to Come  
20 
In addition, the “Frühstartrente” would extend capital
 -
funded provision to children: the state pays 
 EUR
 10 monthly into a depot from age six 
until 18. 
However, the resulting macroeconomic investment volume would be lower than that of the other additional reforms. The Bundesta
 g is 
set to vote on this measure on 16 October 2026.   
21 
EY (2026), European Attractiveness Survey, June 2026. The figures for low
 -
carbon energy, AI and defense refer to Europe as a whole.

### Page 13

13

EUR3.2bn in 2024 and Nvidia is part of a EUR1bn data center project with Deutsche Telekom in Munich.
 22 
AI
-
related 
FDI projects in Europe rose by 96% in 2025, and the number also went up in Germany although total FDI projects 
declined.  
Transport and mobility: 
 Modernizing transport infrastructure is another major investment priority. The Ministry of 
Transport plans to invest EUR166bn between 2025 and 2029, including EUR107bn for rail, EUR52bn for federal 
roads, and EUR8bn for waterways. A key enabler is performa
 nce
-
based concessions. A new generation of public
 -
private partnerships (PPP 2.0) should be based on performance
 -
linked service
 -
level agreements and long
 -
term 
offtake contracts that link investor returns to measurable and predictable cash flows.  
Defense: 
 German defense financing is expected to reach approximately EUR734bn over five years, enabling the 
country to meet NATO's target of spending 3.5% of GDP on core defense by 2029. Planned procurement includes 
land systems, air power, air and missile defense,  
long
 -
range strike capabilities, unmanned systems, munitions, and 
space security assets. Foreign investors are also moving in: the number of defense FDI projects in Europe rose by 
84% in 2025 to 107.
 23 
Only seven of them were in Germany, against 20 in the UK, 14 in France, 12 in Ukraine, 10 in 
Spain and nine in Poland (Figure 14).  Key enabler: simplification. As in other sectors, reducing regulatory and 
procurement complexity will be crucial to acceler
 ating investment and delivery.

What is at stake  
Germany has a rare opportunity to transform today's fiscal stimulus into a sustained investment boom. 
 If 
successful, the country could benefit significantly from a renewed capital influx. Not only would it lift growth from 
the demand side in the short run, it would also raise potential growth and boost long
 -
term growth for decades. The 
benefits would exten
 d beyond Germany 
 – 
Europe cannot afford for its largest economy to remain its weakest link.  
The gains would be economic and political. 
 Stronger growth, functioning infrastructure and rising prosperity would 
help to restore confidence in institutions, strengthening the foundations of the social state and reducing some of the 
economic drivers behind the current political polarization. Howev
 er, successful and bold reforms, as well as 
investors willing to support Germany's renewed momentum, are equally essential to achieving this outcome.  
Failure would come at a high cost. 
 While fiscal spending could provide a temporary boost, structural weaknesses 
would persist, leaving Germany with higher debt and little improvement in long
 -
term growth. The consequences 
would extend beyond the economic sphere, including continued dependenc
 e on foreign energy and technology, 
further erosion of Europe's stability and competitiveness, and continued political polarization and populism. 
Germany's investment challenge is therefore about much more than just growth. It concerns the future economic 
and political resilience of Germany and Europe as a whole.
